⏺ Before planning #7 (US-2, photo-to-listing), I need three decisions from you.
  What would you like to clarify about them?

  So far I've only read code and run read-only commands. Here's what I found:

  - More is built than CLAUDE.md says. The repo already has Vitest, Zod, Prisma
    (with a schema and migrations), Auth.js, Cloudinary (src/lib/cloudinary.ts,
    src/lib/item-photos.ts), guards, and CI.
  - One dependency is missing: the Anthropic SDK (@anthropic-ai/sdk). Installing
    it needs your approval.
  - node_modules isn't installed in this checkout, so I can't run the tests yet.
  - No migration should be needed. I'd hold AI-flagged listings using the
    existing Item.status = PENDING_REVIEW and Item.flagReason fields. The Report
    table can't hold AI flags anyway, because reporterId is required.

  The three decisions:

  1. Upload path. A 5 MB photo is over the 1 MB default size limit for Server
     Actions. We can either raise serverActions.bodySizeLimit in next.config.ts
     (that config change needs your approval) or add a route handler. I'd raise
     the limit, because it matches the pattern the join-community form already
     uses.
  2. When the safety check fails. If Claude times out or returns bad output, the
     listing can either be held for admin review (fail closed) or published
     anyway (fail open). I'd hold it for review.
  3. Prohibited items. Which categories should Claude flag? For example:
     weapons, drugs and alcohol, hazardous materials, and adult or stolen goods.

⏺ npm ci failed because package-lock.json no longer matches package.json. The
  lockfile committed at HEAD is fine. The break comes from changes in your
  working tree that aren't committed yet: git status was clean when this session
  started, and both files have changed since.

  - package.json now lists "@anthropic-ai/sdk": "^1.12.0".
  - package-lock.json gained the SDK and its dependencies (json-schema-to-ts,
    standardwebhooks, …). It also lost three entries:
    - node_modules/@emnapi/core
    - node_modules/@emnapi/runtime
    - node_modules/@emnapi/wasi-threads

  Pranav's last commit (f311425 fix: restore missing Linux dependencies in npm 
  lockfile) added those exact entries back. When npm rewrites the lockfile on
  macOS, it drops optional dependencies it thinks this platform doesn't need.
  npm ci (and CI on Linux) still expects them, so it fails with Missing: 
  @emnapi/wasi-threads@1.2.3.

  To fix it, choose one of these. I can't run either one, because the project
  rules give you all git and install steps.

  1. Keep the SDK and repair the lockfile (what I'd do).
  ! npm install
     Then check that git diff package-lock.json | grep emnapi no longer shows
     the three @emnapi entries as removed. If npm drops them again, run ! npm 
     install --include=optional instead.
  2. Undo the SDK install for now. Run git restore package.json 
     package-lock.json and then npm ci. This gets you back to the committed
     state, but you'll need to add the SDK again before US-2.

  Once npm ci succeeds, npm test will work and I can run the existing test suite
  as a baseline. I'm also taking the SDK install as your approval to add it.

  The three US-2 decisions are still open: the upload path, what happens when
  the safety check fails, and the prohibited-items list. What did you want to
  clarify about them?

✻ Sautéed for 40s · done 5:12 PM

❯ Decisions:                                                                    
                                                                                
  1. Approved: raise serverActions.bodySizeLimit to "6mb" (headroom for         
  multipart                                                                     
     overhead so our validation, not Next's, rejects 5–6 MB files). Add a       
     client-side size check for UX only. Do this change alone, then stop so I   
  can commit.                                                                   
  2. Fail closed. Timeout, refusal, or Zod failure → PENDING_REVIEW with a      
     flagReason. The lender can still fill in the fields manually and save.     
  3. Flag: weapons (incl. replicas/ammo), drugs/paraphernalia,                  
  alcohol/tobacco/vapes,                                                        
     prescription meds, hazardous materials, adult content, ID/personal         
  documents.                                                                    
     Not "stolen goods" (can't be judged from a photo). Use a typed constant of 
     category codes; flagReason stores the code.                                
                                                                                
  Before planning: summarize what src/lib/item-photos.ts and                    
  src/lib/cloudinary.ts                                                         
  already do and which tests cover them. Plan cycles only for new behaviour so  
  every red test genuinely fails.

     Plan: #7 US-2 — List an item from a photo (strict TDD)

     Context

     Issue #7 (owner: Aarushi, Sprint 1, labels tdd/ai/hw4). A lender uploads a
     JPEG/PNG < 5 MB, it's stored in
     Cloudinary, Claude suggests title/category/condition/description within 10
     s, the lender edits and saves,
     prohibited items are held for admin review, and Claude output is
     Zod-validated and fails safely.

     Decisions recorded (from the human, 2026-11-22):
     1. bodySizeLimit raised to "6mb" (multipart headroom so our validation
        rejects 5–6 MB files). Client-side size check is UX only.
     2. Fail closed: timeout, refusal, API error, or Zod failure → listing saves
        as PENDING_REVIEW with a flagReason; lender can still fill fields
        manually.
     3. Prohibited codes (typed constant; Item.flagReason stores the code):
        WEAPON (incl. replicas/ammo), DRUGS (incl. paraphernalia),
        ALCOHOL_TOBACCO (incl. vapes), PRESCRIPTION_MEDS, HAZARDOUS, ADULT,
        ID_DOCUMENTS. No "stolen goods".
        - Confirmed: failures store SAFETY_CHECK_FAILED in flagReason.

     Working rules (from approval):
     - If a RED test passes on first run, stop and report; never alter tests to
       force a failure — the cycle gets re-scoped.
     - RED stubs return placeholders only, no real logic.
     - TEST_DATABASE_URL is set → cycle 11 is a full red-green-refactor cycle.
     - Baseline gate before Step 0: run npm test, confirm prisma/* files now run
       (not skipped) and pass, report counts; stop if any fail.
     - Open items below are out of scope for #7 → list them in the final summary
       as candidate separate issues.

     What already exists (don't re-test)

     - src/lib/cloudinary.ts → uploadImage(buffer, { folder }): folder
       allow-list under borrowloop/ (rejects ../, unknown folders), configures
       from 3 env vars and throws Cloudinary is not configured. Missing: …
       before any network call, uploads via upload_stream with resource_type: 
       "image", returns { url: secure_url, publicId, width, height }. Explicitly
       does no type/size validation. Has a "server-only" comment but no import 
       "server-only". No EXIF stripping.
       - Covered by src/lib/cloudinary.test.ts (5 unit tests, SDK mocked): happy
         path + secure URL, allowed subfolder, folder escape ×4, Cloudinary
         error message, missing credentials. These ran and passed in the 
         baseline.
     - src/lib/item-photos.ts → saveItemPhoto(db, itemId, buffer): calls
       uploadImage, then creates an ItemPhoto at position = count(existing). No
       ownership check (caller's job), no transaction.
       - Covered by src/lib/item-photos.test.ts (3 integration tests: stores
         URL, appends position, no row on upload failure).
         describe.skipIf(!TEST_DATABASE_URL) — skipped in the baseline, so it
         hasn't run locally.
     - Neither helper has any caller in src/app yet. The schema already has
       ItemStatus.PENDING_REVIEW and Item.flagReason → no migration needed.
       Report.reporterId is required, so AI flags live on Item, not Report.
     - Reuse: requireUser (src/server/auth/session.ts), authorizeCommunity /
       getCommunityAccess (src/server/auth/guards.ts, community-access.ts), the
       generic-error Server Action pattern and test style of
       src/app/(app)/communities/actions.ts + join-action.test.ts, the
       injected-client DAL style of src/server/dal/listings.ts, the
       ITEM_CATEGORY_LABELS/ITEM_CONDITION_LABELS enums
       (src/lib/items/labels.ts).

     Because Cloudinary upload and folder safety are already tested, the cycles
     below test only that the new code calls uploadImage (mocked) at the right

     Design (new modules)

     Flow: analyze (upload → validate → Cloudinary + Claude in parallel → create
     DRAFT item holding the photo and server-side flagReason) → client form
     prefilled → save (lender's edits → PUBLISHED, or PENDING_REVIEW if the
     stored draft has a flagReason). The flag lives in the DB from the analyze
     step, so the client can't strip it.

⏺ Stopped: 4 integration tests fail. As you guessed, the test database is 
  missing a migration. I haven't run any migration.

  npm test with .env.test loaded: 31 files passed and 2 failed. 442 tests
  passed, 4 failed, and 7 were skipped (453 in total).

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │                   File                   │            Result            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ prisma/admin-access, joining, listings,  │ Now run and pass. Before,    │
  │ membership, src/lib/item-photos          │ these were skipped.          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ prisma/account.test.ts                   │ 4 of 4 failed                │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │                                          │ Suite failed in setup (its 7 │
  │ prisma/auth-adapter.test.ts              │  tests show as skipped       │
  │                                          │ because beforeAll threw)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  Why: both failures have the same root cause:

  The table `public.Account` does not exist in the current database.
    at prisma/auth-adapter.test.ts:112 (db.user.deleteMany in cleanUp)

  The Account table comes from the second migration,
  prisma/migrations/20261122120000_add_auth_account. Everything that passes only
  uses tables from the first migration, 20261121210255_init. So the test
  database has init applied but not add_auth_account. I didn't run prisma 
  migrate status to confirm it, because pointing Prisma at the test database
  would mean using the URL from .env.test.

  To fix it (needs your approval, and you may prefer to run it yourself): apply
  the pending migration to the test database only. prisma.config.ts loads .env
  through dotenv/config, which by default doesn't overwrite a variable that's
  already set, so this should target the test database:

  ! DATABASE_URL="$(grep '^TEST_DATABASE_URL=' .env.test | cut -d= -f2-)" npx 
  prisma migrate deploy

  Check that prisma.config.ts reads DATABASE_URL before you run it. Use migrate 
  deploy, not migrate dev: it only applies the existing migration files and
  never generates new ones or resets the database.

  Also seen: pg prints a warning that sslmode=require is treated as verify-full.
  It's harmless for now. If you want to silence it, set sslmode=verify-full in
  the .env.test URL. That's your file to edit.

  Changed file: vitest.config.mts only. It loads just TEST_DATABASE_URL from
  .env.test (explicit path, parsed into a separate object, a value set in the
  shell wins, and the suites skip if the file is missing). next.config.ts is
  untouched.

  Two options:
  - Commit now (chore(test): load .env.test for integration tests). The config
    change is correct, and the failures are about the database's state, not the
    code.
  - Or apply the migration first. Then I'll rerun the suite to confirm
    everything is green, and you commit after that.

  Either way, I'll start Step 0 only after your commit.

⏺ Step 0 is done: Server Actions now accept request bodies up to 6 MB. Stopping
  here for your commit.

  What changed: only next.config.ts. I added
  experimental.serverActions.bodySizeLimit: "6mb" with a short comment. In this
  Next.js version the option sits under experimental; I checked both the bundled
  docs and the type definitions.

  One correction to the plan's security note. Next.js reads the whole request
  body before the action's own code runs. So even a signed-out request can make
  the server read up to 6 MB before requireUser rejects it. The plan said
  authentication on every action mitigates this; it only partly does. The
  comment in the file now says so accurately. If you want to limit that
  exposure, rate limiting is already on the out-of-scope list.

  Issue: #7 US-2: List an item from a photo (cycle 1, upload validation)

  Result: all 11 tests pass, I didn't touch the test file, and the full suite,
  lint and type check are clean.

  Commands and output:

  npx vitest run src/server/images/validate-upload.test.ts
   Test Files  1 passed (1)
        Tests  11 passed (11)

  npm test
   Test Files  34 passed (34)
        Tests  464 passed (464)

  npm run lint printed no errors or warnings, and npx tsc --noEmit was clean.

  How validateUpload works now:
  1. Size first: file.size >= 5 MiB → "Please upload a photo under 5 MB.".
     Nothing is read from the file.
  2. Header only: it reads file.slice(0, 8), the length of the longer signature
     (PNG's 8 bytes), never the whole file.
  3. Signature match: JPEG FF D8 FF or PNG 89 50 4E 47 0D 0A 1A 0A. Anything
     else, including empty or truncated files, → "Please upload a JPEG or PNG 
     photo.".
  4. Declared type must match: file.type has to equal the detected type, so JPEG
     bytes declared as image/png are rejected with the same message.

  Worth discussing at REFACTOR or later:
  - The declared-type match is strict. That's what your test asks for (JPEG
    bytes sent as image/png are rejected). But a browser or tool that sends an
    empty type or image/jpg for a real JPEG would also be rejected. The usual
    browsers send image/jpeg for .jpg files, so I left it as is. If you want to
    allow an empty declared type, that needs a new RED test first.
  - MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES is defined in this server-only file. Cycle 2 needs the same
    limit in the client-safe src/lib/validation/listing.ts. The refactor or
    cycle 2 can move it there so both import one constant.

  Files changed:
  - src/server/images/validate-upload.ts (modified: placeholder replaced with
    the implementation)

  Suggested commit message:
  feat(upload): reject non-JPEG/PNG and 5 MB+ photos by magic bytes (green) (#7)

  Result: all 4 new tests fail on assertions, because the placeholder never

  What the claude-api skill recommends (nothing calls the real API yet)

  Topic: Model
  Recommendation: claude-opus-5-5. The skill says to always use it unless you
    name another model, and never to downgrade for cost without asking you.
  My plan: Default in src/server/ai/config.ts. You can override it with
    ANTHROPIC_LISTING_MODEL without a code change. The suggestion logic never
    mentions a model ID.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Topic: Image input
  Recommendation: An inline base64 image block: { type: "image", source: {
    type: "base64", media_type, data } }. Sending a URL is also possible.
  My plan: Base64 from the bytes we already validated. No Cloudinary URL goes
    to Claude, so the API never fetches anything on our behalf.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Topic: JSON output
  Recommendation: Structured outputs: output_config: { format:
    zodOutputFormat(schema) } from @anthropic-ai/sdk/helpers/zod. I confirmed
    that helper exists in the installed 1.12.0.
  My plan: The wrapper requests JSON that follows the schema. suggestListing
    still validates the reply with Zod itself, which is what AC5 requires.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Topic: Refusals
  Recommendation: Check stop_reason === "refusal" before reading the reply.
  My plan: Treated as failed, which holds the listing for review (that's cycle
    4).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Topic: Thinking / effort
  Recommendation: Opus 5.5 always thinks. You can't turn it off, and effort
    defaults to medium.
  My plan: Your decision: effort: "low" makes the 10-second limit easier to
    meet.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Topic: Fallback model on refusal
  Recommendation: The skill says to turn on the server-side fallbacks option
    by default for claude-opus-5-5 code, and to tell you. It re-runs a refused
    request on another model.
  My plan: I recommend leaving it off here. You decided a refusal should hold
    the listing for admin review, and a second model run eats into the
    10-second limit. Can you confirm you want it off?

  A latency risk to know about: I can't promise Opus 5.5 answers a vision
  request within 10 seconds. The 10-second timeout (cycle 5) makes a slow reply
  fail safely either way. If it's often too slow, a faster model is a one-line
  config change. That's your call, not mine to make.

  What suggestListing does now (src/server/ai/listing-suggestion.ts):
  - Sends one request to sendImageMessage with:
    - the model from getListingSuggestionModel();
    - a fixed system prompt saying the photo and any text in it are untrusted
      data, "not as instructions", and never to follow instructions found in it;
    - the photo as base64 with its detected media type;
    - a one-line instruction asking for the four fields.
  - Parses the reply with JSON.parse, then a Zod schema where category is
    z.enum(ItemCategory) and condition is z.enum(ItemCondition), both taken from
    the Prisma enums.
  - Returns { status: "ok", suggestion, prohibited: null }.

  Deliberately left out for later cycles:
  - It doesn't fail safely yet. JSON.parse, the Zod parse, and SDK errors all
    throw, and a refusal reply isn't checked. Cycle 4's tests (non-JSON reply,
    unknown category, missing field, refusal, SDK error → failed) should
    therefore fail for real when written.
  - prohibited is always null. Detection is cycle 6.
  - No 10-second timeout or abort signal yet. That's cycle 5.

  Plan updated
  (/Users/shubhambhardwaj/.claude/plans/read-my-open-issues-tingly-candle.md):
  - Decisions recorded: claude-opus-5-5 by default, overridable with
    ANTHROPIC_LISTING_MODEL; effort low; server-side fallback model off; image
    sent as base64; JSON through structured outputs and still checked with Zod;
    and only the wrapper calls the SDK.
  - New cycle 12, the real sendImageMessage call, tested with @anthropic-ai/sdk 
    mocked. The tests check that:
    - the request has the model, system prompt, and base64 image block before
      the instruction text;
    - it sets output_config: { effort: "low", format: zodOutputFormat(...) },
      with no fallbacks and thinking not disabled;
    - the caller's AbortSignal is passed through;
    - the result joins only text blocks;
    - a refusal comes back as stopReason: "refusal" instead of throwing;
    - SDK errors propagate unchanged.
  - Scheduled after cycle 6 and before the Server Actions (cycles 9–10). Passing
    the Zod schema to the wrapper may mean adding an outputSchema field to its
    request. That would be a REFACTOR step with the tests kept green.

  Why it fails (expected). Every failure is an AssertionError, and they fall
  into two groups:

  What happens today: The function throws instead of returning failed
  Cases: invalid JSON, unknown category or condition, 4 missing fields, SDK
    error
  Example message: promise rejected "SyntaxError: Unexpected token 'S',
    "Sure!…" instead of resolving, promise rejected "ZodError: […]", promise
    rejected "Error: 529 overloaded_error"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What happens today: It returns ok for output it should reject
  Cases: empty after trimming ×2, too long ×2, refusal
  Example message: expected { status: 'ok', …(2) } to strictly equal { status:
    'failed' }

  The two leak tests fail because today the function throws, and the thrown
  error carries the injected marker (marker-7f3a) or the fake key
  (sk-ant-secret-marker). Each test also checks that the result is exactly { 
  status: "failed" } and contains neither string.

  How the cases are built:
  - Every failure case uses toStrictEqual({ status: "failed" }), so an extra
    field such as error, raw or reason would also fail the test. That covers
    your "no raw model text in the result" requirement.
  - The refusal case sends valid JSON on purpose. So GREEN has to check
    stopReason; a failed parse can't catch the refusal by accident.

    passed, 15 failed.

  They sit in their own describe block, labelled "characterization", with a
  comment saying they were added after cycle 4's GREEN step and pass by design.
  That way the TDD history stays honest. The limits are imported from
  src/lib/validation/listing.ts, the same constants the implementation uses.

  What they protect against: an off-by-one change such as .max(TITLE_MAX - 1),
  or replacing .max with a "less than" check, would fail the two boundary tests.
  Removing .trim() would fail the trimming test, and so would checking the
  length before trimming. Together with cycle 4's 101 and 1,001-character
  rejection tests, both sides of each limit are now covered. I didn't
  temporarily break the code to watch these fail, because you said not to change
  the implementation. If you want that proof, I can do it on a throwaway change
  and revert it.

  What suggestListing looks like now:
  let reply;
  try {
    reply = await sendImageMessage({ ... });
  } catch {
    return FAILED;                       // failure path 1: the call threw
  }

  const suggestion = parseSuggestion(reply);
  if (!suggestion) {
    return FAILED;                       // failure path 2: untrusted reply
  }
  return { status: "ok", suggestion, prohibited: null };

  parseSuggestion is a private function (not exported). It returns null for a
  refusal, for text that isn't JSON, and when the schema check fails. Otherwise
  it returns the parsed, trimmed fields. The only other edit is a type-only
  import of ImageMessageResult from anthropic-client.

  live in config.ts, because the tests replace that module with a mock, so an
  import from it would be undefined. The tests import the constant and use it
  everywhere instead of a literal 10,000.

           ← test 2, line 213
  AssertionError: expected undefined to be an instance of AbortSignal           
           ← test 3, line 231

  Why each test fails (expected):

  Test: 1. Waiting at 9,999 ms, failed at 10,000 ms
  Fails at: the 10,000 ms check
  Reason: No timeout exists, so suggestListing is still pending. The 9,999 ms
    check before it passes.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: 2. The signal is aborted at the timeout
  Fails at: the first check on the signal
  Reason: No AbortSignal is passed to sendImageMessage yet.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Test: 3. An in-time reply returns ok, and nothing fires later
  Fails at: the signal check
  Reason: The status: "ok" checks before it pass, because the current code
    already returns ok. It then fails because no signal is passed, so there's
    nothing to show stays un-aborted.

  Why I combined the 9,999 ms and 10,000 ms cases: you listed "at 9,999 ms it
  hasn't returned yet" as its own case. On its own it would pass today, because
  with no timeout the call never returns at all. Your rule says to stop if a RED
  test passes on its first run, so I made it the first assertion of the timeout
  test. That test now checks both sides of the boundary and fails for the right
  reason. If you'd rather have them separate, I'll split them out, and the
  9,999 ms test will pass by design, like the guard cases in cycle 2.

  Tests 1 and 3 also contain assertions that already pass (the 9,999 ms check,
  and the "returns ok" check). They're there to pin the boundary and the success
  path. Each test still fails on what the cycle adds: the timeout, the abort
  signal, and the cleared timer.

  How the tests work:
  - vi.useFakeTimers() runs before each test in the new block, and
    vi.useRealTimers() after.
  - "Never resolves" is a promise that's never settled. A small track() helper
    records whether suggestListing has finished without awaiting it, so a
    missing timeout fails the assertion instead of hanging the test.
  - "Nothing fires afterward" checks that vi.getTimerCount() is 0 once the reply
    arrives. It then moves time forward another 10 seconds and checks the
    signal is still not aborted.

  Note for GREEN: the timeout has to use setTimeout with an AbortController.
  AbortSignal.timeout() doesn't run on Vitest's fake timers, so these tests
  wouldn't see it fire.

  Issue: #7 US-2: List an item from a photo (cycle 5, 10-second timeout)

  Result: all 3 timeout tests pass, along with the other 22 in the file, the
  full suite, lint and type check. I didn't touch the tests.

  Commands and output:

  npx vitest run --reporter=verbose src/server/ai/listing-suggestion.test.ts
   ✓ suggestListing timeout > is still waiting at 1 ms before the timeout, then 
  returns { status: 'failed' } at the timeout
   ✓ suggestListing timeout > aborts the signal passed to sendImageMessage when 
  the timeout fires
   ✓ suggestListing timeout > returns ok for a reply that arrives in time, and 
  clears the timer so nothing fires afterward
   Test Files  1 passed (1)
        Tests  25 passed (25)

  npm test
   Test Files  36 passed (36)
        Tests  493 passed (493)

  npm run lint printed no errors or warnings, and npx tsc --noEmit was clean.

  What changed in suggestListing:
  - The timer: an AbortController plus a setTimeout for SUGGESTION_TIMEOUT_MS.
    When it fires, it calls controller.abort() and settles a "timed_out"
    promise. AbortSignal.timeout() isn't used.
  - The call: sendImageMessage now receives signal: controller.signal, and its
    result is raced against the timeout with Promise.race.
    - If the timeout wins, the function returns FAILED.
    - If the call throws, it still returns FAILED, as before.
    - Otherwise the reply goes on to parseSuggestion, unchanged.
  - Cleanup: clearTimeout(timer) runs in a finally block, so the timer is
    cleared whichever way the call ends. That's what keeps vi.getTimerCount() at
    0 and the signal un-aborted in the in-time test.
  - The doc comment now describes the timeout and the abort.

  Why the race is needed, not just the abort: aborting only helps if
  sendImageMessage listens to the signal. The current wrapper is still a
  placeholder, and the test mock never settles. So suggestListing has to stop
  waiting on its own. When the real SDK call (cycle 12) rejects after the abort,
  that late rejection lands on the call that already lost the race.
  Promise.race already handles it, so it won't show up as an unhandled
  rejection.

  - src/server/ai/listing-suggestion.ts (modified)

  skip it: the block is about 20 lines and the finally keeps it clear. After
  that comes cycle 6 RED, which detects prohibited items such as WEAPON and
  rejects unknown codes.

  Issue: #7 US-2: List an item from a photo (cycle 5, 10-second timeout)

  Result: the timeout now lives in withTimeout(run, ms) in
  src/server/ai/with-timeout.ts, and suggestListing reads as call, check for a
  timeout, parse. Behaviour is unchanged, the tests are untouched, and
  everything is green.

  Commands and output:

  npm test
   Test Files  36 passed (36)
        Tests  493 passed (493)

  npm run lint printed no errors or warnings, and npx tsc --noEmit was clean.

  What the new helper does (src/server/ai/with-timeout.ts, server-only):
  withTimeout<T>(run: (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T>, ms): Promise<T | 
  "timed_out">
  - It runs run with a fresh AbortController signal and races it against a
    setTimeout(ms) that aborts the signal and resolves to "timed_out".
  - If run throws, the error propagates unchanged, so suggestListing still
    returns FAILED from its existing catch.
  - clearTimeout runs in a finally block, so nothing fires after it returns.
  - It uses setTimeout rather than AbortSignal.timeout() so fake timers work.
    The doc comment says so.

  suggestListing now:
  let reply;
  try {
    reply = await withTimeout((signal) => sendImageMessage({ ..., signal }),
  SUGGESTION_TIMEOUT_MS);
  } catch {
    return FAILED;                 // the call threw
  }

  if (reply === "timed_out") {
    return FAILED;                 // the 10-second timeout fired
  }

  const suggestion = parseSuggestion(reply);
  if (!suggestion) {
    return FAILED;                 // the reply can't be trusted
  }
  return { status: "ok", suggestion, prohibited: null };

  Coverage check: I ran the suggestion tests with coverage on src/server/ai/.
  - with-timeout.ts and listing-suggestion.ts: fully covered. The text report
    only lists files that aren't, and neither appeared.
  - anthropic-client.ts (the placeholder): 0%, because the tests mock it. Cycle
    12 will test it.
  - config.ts: 0%, mocked for the same reason. This is the untested
    configuration I mentioned at cycle 3.

  Two small notes:
  - withTimeout has no test of its own. The three timeout tests cover it through
    suggestListing, as you said. If another feature reuses it, it deserves
    direct tests then.
  - The "timed_out" string is a marker. It can't be confused with a real result
    here, because ImageMessageResult is an object. A future caller whose own
    result could be that exact string would need a different marker, such as a
    symbol.

    timer code removed)

  Why each one fails (expected): every failure is an AssertionError. The Zod
  schema doesn't know about prohibited, so it silently drops the field and the
  result always has prohibited: null.

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │                    │ dropped and null comes back.                       │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Unknown or invalid │ expected { status: 'ok', …(2) } to strictly equal  │
  │  code ×5, missing  │ { status: 'failed' }. A dropped field can't fail   │

    maps only some of the codes.

    must give failed.

    reserved for the server, so Claude mustn't be able to set it.

    GREEN can list the codes in either one.

  (Vitest prints each identical message once, so the counts add up to fewer than
  12.)

  Why it fails (expected):
  - The stubs never touch the database, so every check on a database call fails.
  - createDraftListing returns { id: "" } instead of the new draft's id.
  - publishDraftListing returns { status: "PUBLISHED" }. I chose that
    placeholder so the "not_found" tests fail. A stub returning "not_found"
    would have made four of them pass on their first run.

  What the tests check:

  Function: createDraftListing
  Checks: ownerId comes from the session user even when the input carries
    ownerId: "attacker-id". Status is DRAFT even when the input says
    PUBLISHED. The given flagReason is saved. Fields and community are saved.
    The photo is a nested photos.create with position: 0 (the cover). It
    returns { id }.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Function: publishDraftListing
  Checks: Both the read and the update use the scope { id, ownerId: user.id,
    status: DRAFT, community: { memberships: { some: { userId: user.id } } }
    }. The update's data is exactly the 4 edited fields plus status, so an
    injected ownerId, communityId, flagReason or status can't get through.
    Status follows the stored flagReason (null → PUBLISHED; WEAPON or
    SAFETY_CHECK_FAILED → PENDING_REVIEW), even when the input says
    flagReason: null, status: PUBLISHED. "not_found" with no update when the
    read finds nothing. "not_found" when the update changes 0 rows.

  Design points for you to check before GREEN:

  1. decideListingModeration doesn't fit this step. You asked for the status to
     come "via decideListingModeration", but that function takes an AI result
     (ListingSuggestionResult | null), while publish only has the flagReason
     stored on the draft. Rebuilding a fake AI result from a stored flag would
     be awkward. So the tests check the outcome, not which helper is used. For
     GREEN, I suggest a small pure helper in listing-moderation.ts, for example
     statusForFlagReason(flagReason) (null → PUBLISHED, anything else →
     PENDING_REVIEW). decideListingModeration would then call it too, so the
     rule lives in one place. Do you want that?
  2. The tests fix how publish talks to the database: one item.findFirst to read
     the stored flagReason, then one item.updateMany with the same scope. If
     updateMany changes 0 rows, publish returns "not_found". That also stops a
     draft from being published twice. I chose updateMany over update because it
     returns a row count and doesn't throw when nothing matches.
  3. createDraftListing doesn't check membership itself. A Prisma create can't
     be limited to communities the user belongs to, the way a where clause can.
     The Server Action (cycle 9) checks access with getCommunityAccess first. If
     you want the data layer to defend itself too, createDraftListing could
     look up the membership inside a transaction before creating. That would be
     a new RED test here. Should I add it?
  4. The three "not_found" scenarios look the same with a fake. Another user's
     draft, an already-published draft, and a removed member are each just "no
     row matches". These unit tests prove the scope is in the query. Cycle 11
     proves the scope actually excludes those rows in a real database.

  src/server/dal/listing-drafts.ts:

    - It looks up tx.membership.findUnique({ where: { userId_communityId: { 
      userId: user.id, communityId } }, select: { id: true } }). With no
      membership, it returns "not_found".
    - Otherwise it calls tx.item.create with ownerId: user.id, status: DRAFT,
      the given flagReason, the four fields, and the photo as a nested create
      with position: 0. It returns { id }.
    - It reads each input field by name, so injected ownerId or status values
      are never written.

    status: DRAFT, and a membership for user.id in the item's community. Then:
    - findFirst({ where, select: { flagReason: true } }). Nothing found →
      "not_found".
    - status = statusForFlagReason(draft.flagReason).
    - updateMany({ where, data: { title, description, category, condition, 
      status } }). A count of 0 → "not_found".
    - Otherwise it returns { status }.

  The change (src/server/domain/listing-moderation.ts, 3 lines added and 7
  removed):
  export function decideListingModeration(result: ListingSuggestionResult |
  null): ListingModeration {
    const flagReason: ListingFlagReason | null =
      result === null || result.status === "failed" ? SAFETY_CHECK_FAILED :
  result.prohibited;
    return { status: statusForFlagReason(flagReason), flagReason };
  }
  decideListingModeration now only works out the flag reason:
  SAFETY_CHECK_FAILED when the check failed or didn't run, otherwise the
  prohibited code or null. Choosing the status from that flag is left to
  statusForFlagReason. That's the same rule publishDraftListing applies to the
  flag stored on the draft.

  Coverage: I ran coverage on the two files with their tests. Both are fully
  covered (18/18 statements, 14/14 branches, 5/5 functions). statusForFlagReason
  is now exercised by cycle 7's 10 moderation tests as well as the publish
  tests. That closes the gap I mentioned at GREEN.

     JSON check and hold the listing for review.
  2. The API key is passed explicitly: new Anthropic({ apiKey: 
     process.env.ANTHROPIC_API_KEY }). On the server this means only the
     environment variable is used, never a local ant auth profile. If the key is
     missing, the SDK fails when the first request is made. That error
     propagates, and suggestListing already turns it into { status: "failed" }
     (fail closed).

  What src/server/ai/anthropic-client.ts does now:
  - The client: getClient() creates new Anthropic({ apiKey: 
    process.env.ANTHROPIC_API_KEY }) on first use and caches it in the module.
    Importing the file never needs a key.
  - The request: messages.create(params, { signal: request.signal }), where
    params has:
    - the given model and system, and max_tokens: 16_000;
    - one user message whose content is the base64 image block, then the
      instruction text;
    - output_config: { effort: "low" }, plus format: 
      zodOutputFormat(outputSchema) when the caller passes a schema.

    It doesn't send thinking (Opus 5.5 always thinks) or fallbacks.
  - The result: the text of the reply's text blocks, joined, plus
    message.stop_reason. A refusal comes back as data, and SDK errors aren't
    caught.

  Before this goes live:
  1. suggestListing doesn't send its schema yet. outputSchema is still optional,
     and nothing passes it, so production requests currently go out without a
     structured-output format. Your own Zod check still catches bad output and
     fails closed, but Claude isn't being held to the JSON shape. The REFACTOR
     step we discussed fixes this: pass suggestionSchema from suggestListing,
     then make the field required.
  2. A missing API key isn't strictly limited to the environment variable. If
     ANTHROPIC_API_KEY is unset, apiKey is undefined, and the SDK falls back to
     its normal lookup (ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN, then a local ant auth login
     profile). On Vercel neither exists, so the request fails and the listing is
     held for review. On a developer's machine with a profile, it would quietly
     use that profile. If you want "environment variable only" enforced,
     getClient() could throw a fixed error when the key is missing. That would
     be a small new RED test.
  3. Nothing has called the real API yet. These tests prove the request is built
     to match the 1.12.0 types. Whether the live API accepts it (model, image,
     effort and the JSON format together) needs one manual call with a real key.
     Every such call is billed, so I won't make it without your go-ahead.

  "No behaviour change" holds for everything the tests can see, but not for the 
  request sent to Claude. Production requests now include the structured-output
  format they were missing before. That was the point of this step, and it's
  invisible to the mocked tests.

  What that format contains: I printed what zodOutputFormat produces for the
  same schema, using a throwaway script in the scratchpad, not the repo:
  - category, condition and prohibited go through as real enums. prohibited is
    anyOf: [enum, null].
  - All five fields are required, with additionalProperties: false.
  - The length limits don't go through as hard limits. The SDK turns min(1) and
    max(100) / max(1000) into a hint in the field's description ("{minLength: 1,
    maxLength: 100}"), because the structured-output format doesn't enforce
    length keywords. The same happens to .trim(). So Claude can still return an
    over-long title, and your own Zod check after the call is what enforces the
    limits. Keeping that check was the right call.

  The change, in getClient() in src/server/ai/anthropic-client.ts:
  const apiKey = process.env.ANTHROPIC_API_KEY?.trim();
  if (!apiKey) {
    throw new Error("Claude is not configured: ANTHROPIC_API_KEY is not set.");
  }
  client ??= new Anthropic({ apiKey });
  - The check runs on every call, before any client exists. With no key, nothing
    is created and no request is made, so the SDK can't fall back to
    ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN or a local profile.
  - The message names the variable and never includes a value.
  - The key is trimmed before use. So a whitespace-only key counts as missing,
    and stray spaces around a real key don't reach the SDK.
  - The doc comment explains why.
  - In practice, suggestListing catches this error like any other and returns { 
    status: "failed" }, so the listing is held for review.

  What analyzePhotoAction does now, in the order you gave:
  1. Auth check and community guard: one call to the existing US-1
     getCommunityAccess. It does the session check (requireUser) and then the
     membership check. It receives only { communityId: 
     formData.get("communityId") }, level: "member", and callbackPath: 
     COMMUNITIES_PATH (the same path the join action uses). A signed-out user's
     redirect propagates. Any decision other than allow returns the generic
     error.
  2. Validation: a missing or non-file photo gets "Please upload a JPEG or PNG
     photo." Otherwise validateUpload(photo) runs, and its message is returned
     on failure.
  3. In parallel: Promise.all([uploadImage(Buffer.from(bytes)), suggestListing({
     bytes, mime })]).
  4. decideListingModeration(result) gives the flagReason.

  real field names they give the same answer. hasOwn additionally rejects names
  inherited from Object.prototype, such as "toString". Zod would never produce
  those, so no test sees a difference.

  Two positive controls, so the "not_found" results aren't empty wins:
  - creating a draft writes DRAFT with the right owner and community, and the
    photo at position: 0;
  - publishing a clean draft gives PUBLISHED with the edits.

  These also confirm, against real Postgres, the two points I couldn't prove
  with fakes in cycle 8: the interactive transaction, and the relation filter
  (community.memberships.some) inside updateMany's where.

  Following the existing prisma/*.test.ts pattern:
  - Skipping: describe.skipIf(!TEST_DATABASE_URL), and vi.mock("server-only").
  - Fresh fixtures for every test: 3 users and 2 communities.
  - Cleanup only touches this file's rows. Each run gets a random cy11-xxxxxxxx
    prefix, and cleanup deletes only communities and users with that prefix;
    their memberships, items and photos go with them through the cascades. It
    runs before each test, in afterEach (which runs even when an assertion
    fails), and in afterAll, which also calls $disconnect. I didn't separately
    query the database afterwards for leftovers. The cleanup is the same
    mechanism the existing integration tests use.
  - No global client: the data-layer functions take a getClient argument, so the
    tests pass () => db instead of setting it.

  Because the page imports the Server Actions, this build also ran Next's check
  that actions.ts only exports async functions. The cycle 9 build couldn't do
  that, and it passes now.

  What I built:
  - page.tsx: a static shell with a breadcrumb and a <Suspense> fallback.
    Nothing is cached; there's no "use cache" anywhere.
  - new-listing-content.tsx: follows community-listings-content.tsx. It calls
    connection(), then the existing getCommunityAccess guard at member level,
    then notFound() unless access is allowed. Only the validated community id
    goes to the form, and both actions check access again themselves.
    - The proxy needed no change: it already treats /c/<id>/items/new as a
      member route.
  - new-listing-form.tsx: a client component with useActionState for each step.
    - Step 1: a file input limited to image/jpeg,image/png. On change and on
      submit, isPhotoTooLarge shows "This photo is 5 MB or larger…" and stops
      the upload. Server errors show in an alert, and the button reads "Looking
      at your photo…" while it runs.
    - Step 2: the title, description, category and condition fields, prefilled
      from Claude's suggestion and keyed by draft id.
      - With manual entry, a note says suggestions weren't available, and
        category and condition start on a disabled "Choose…" option.
      - Field errors appear under each field, linked with aria-invalid and
        aria-describedby.
      - The fields are controlled, because React 19 clears uncontrolled fields
        after each submit, which would wipe the lender's edits on a validation
        error.
    - After saving: "Your listing is live", or "Your listing is awaiting review
      — A community admin will review it…", with no reason given, plus a link
      back to the community.
    - Every input has a <label>, and all text renders as plain text. There's no
      dangerouslySetInnerHTML in the folder.
  - Small supporting changes:
    - communityPathFor accepts "/items/new" so sign-in returns the user to the
      right page. That's a type-only change.
    - The category and condition dropdowns use small type guards instead of
      casts.

  access to .env.example. That matches the project rule that keeps me out of

  Here's what to add, so you can paste it in yourself:

  # --- Anthropic (Claude) — photo-to-listing suggestions (US-2) 
  -----------------
  # Server-only. Never prefix with NEXT_PUBLIC_ and never commit a real key.
  # Get a key from https://platform.claude.com/ (Settings → API keys).
  # Required: if it's unset or blank, the app doesn't call Claude at all.
  # Listings are then held for admin review (fail closed).
  ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=sk-ant-your-key-here

  # Optional: the Claude model for listing suggestions.
  # Defaults to claude-opus-5-5 when unset (src/server/ai/config.ts).
  # ANTHROPIC_LISTING_MODEL=claude-opus-5-5

  Where each value is read:
  - ANTHROPIC_API_KEY: read only on the server, in getClient() in
    src/server/ai/anthropic-client.ts. That file imports server-only, so it
    can't end up in the browser bundle. The comments explain what happens when
    the key is missing, so the next person isn't surprised when everything goes
    to review.
  - ANTHROPIC_LISTING_MODEL: optional and commented out, because the default in
    config.ts is enough for now.

  Two places that need the real key, not this file:
  - Your local .env, to try photo-to-listing on your machine with real calls.
  - Vercel's project settings, for deployed environments.

  Don't add the key to .env.test. Unit and integration tests mock the SDK, and
  CI shouldn't have a real key.

  If you'd like me to make this kind of edit in future, you'd need to add a
  permission for .env.example only in .claude/settings.json. Keep .env and
  .env.test denied.
